Ever since the night my cousin died in the highway collision. Which, by the way, has been an absolute nightmare to deal with administratively. The state police took over two weeks just to finalize the official accident report. I had to have my general counsel hound the precinct just to get the basic paperwork moving so we could start sorting out his estate. We pay exorbitant local taxes, and the municipal infrastructure is just shockingly inefficient. I mean, if my operations team took two weeks to generate a standard incident report, I'd clear out the entire department. You have to have standardized, automated protocols for these things. I actually told the mayor at a charity fundraiser last year that the city needs to completely overhaul and digitize their records department, but dealing with local government is like talking to a brick wall. They have absolutely no concept of basic operational efficiency... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, how long I've been feeling this way. Right. Yes, it's been about three weeks, since the night of the collision.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have been able to enjoy or look forward to since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Enjoy? No, I... I really don't think so. I actually had my executive assistant clear my calendar of all the social engagements for the month, which was a massive undertaking. She had to cancel my attendance at the Women in Leadership summit, which I was supposed to keynote. I had this whole speech prepared about navigating corporate restructuring during economic downturns. It's a very timely topic, considering the current interest rates and how supply chain disruptions are forcing companies to pivot their entire distribution models. I was going to use our acquisition of that logistics firm in Atlanta as a case study. We managed to integrate their entire routing software into our mainframe in under ninety days, which was a logistical miracle, frankly. The project manager on that integration, David, he's brilliant, I'm probably going to promote him to VP of Operations by the end of the fiscal year... \n\nBut I'm sorry, you asked about enjoying things. Look forward to things. No. Like I mentioned before, even with my family around... like during the prayers, or when my nieces were trying so hard to be sweet and comfort me... I just couldn't feel it. I went through the motions, I behaved the way you're supposed to behave, but there was just zero warmth or... or relief inside. It's like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain just shut off. So no, there's really nothing I'm enjoying right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find interesting or enjoyable before your cousin's death that you have still been doing, even if you don't enjoy them as much?\n\nPatient: Activities I still do? Well, I suppose I'm still attending the family gatherings and the prayer circles for him. We had to organize quite a few of them. I actually ended up having to coordinate the catering for the last one myself because my sister-in-law was completely overwhelmed. I tried to use my usual corporate caterer—they do these incredible artisanal spreads and are completely zero-waste, which is a major sustainability initiative I've been pushing at the company. I actually mandated that all our executive retreats be zero-waste by Q3. It's not just about optics; the cost savings on disposable materials alone justified the switch within six months. I had my procurement team run the numbers, and the ROI was undeniable. If you don't track the metrics, you can't manage the outcome, that's what I always tell my department heads... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. You asked what I'm still doing even if I don't enjoy it. \n\nSo, yes, I still go to those prayer gatherings. But like I've been telling you, it's completely hollow. I know I sound like a broken record today, but even when I'm sitting there and my nieces are hugging me and trying so hard to comfort me... I'm just going through the motions. I'm participating in the activity, I'm behaving properly as the matriarch of the family, but that's all it is. I feel absolutely zero warmth or relief from it. It's just... quiet. That whole affectionate part of me is just quiet.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to experience any kind of pleasure at all, even in small things?\n\nPatient: Small things? Like... well, normally I'd say my morning espresso. I had a custom La Marzocco machine installed in the executive suite last year during our floor renovation. The renovation itself was a complete headache, by the way. The interior design firm we contracted kept trying to push this open-concept layout, which I absolutely vetoed. Open concept is terrible for executive confidentiality, and the acoustics are a nightmare for international conference calls. I had to personally step in and redesign the floor plan with the architects to ensure the C-suite had proper soundproofing. We ended up sourcing these incredible acoustic glass panels from a supplier in Germany, which delayed the project by three weeks because of customs, but it was absolutely worth it. You have to stand your ground with contractors or they will just walk all over your vision and stick you with a subpar product... \n\nBut anyway, small things. Pleasure. No. Honestly, it's exactly what I was just telling you a moment ago. I know I'm repeating myself, but whether it's a cup of coffee or those prayer gatherings with my family, the result is the same. Even when my nieces were right there, hugging me and trying to comfort me, I was just behaving properly. Doing what's expected. There's just no warmth or relief inside me at all. It really is like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain has just gone completely quiet. So no, not even the small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you could have done something to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? No, I... I don't see how I could have. He was in another state when the accident happened, and I was in the middle of our quarterly earnings call. It's funny you bring up prevention, actually, because risk management is a huge part of my job. Just last quarter, I had our legal team completely overhaul our vendor liability contracts because one of our suppliers in Southeast Asia was cutting corners on safety protocols. I told the board, you cannot outsource liability. If a factory goes up in flames, the press doesn't care that it was a third-party contractor; they care whose logo is on the shipping crates. We ended up switching to a more expensive supplier in Vietnam, which took a hit on our margins in Q2, but the long-term risk mitigation is invaluable. I'm actually flying out there next month to personally audit their facilities, assuming I can get my calendar sorted out... \n\nBut no, regarding my cousin. I don't have those kinds of thoughts. I know I couldn't have stopped it. It was just a terrible accident.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any pleasure or enjoyment when you are doing things that you normally would find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Pleasure or enjoyment? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? I know my mind has been a bit scattered lately, but I'm pretty sure I just said... \n\nLook, it's the same thing I was just explaining about the prayer gatherings. It doesn't matter what the activity is. Even when my nieces were right there, literally wrapping their arms around me and trying so hard to comfort me, I felt absolutely nothing. I was just... behaving properly. Doing the right things, saying the right things, but feeling zero warmth or relief inside. It's exactly like I said, that whole affectionate, positive part of me has just gone completely quiet. So no, I'm not feeling any enjoyment, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel empty or numb inside most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Empty or numb? Yes, I suppose that's the best way to describe it. Like I was just saying... I feel like a broken record here, but it's exactly what happened at those prayer gatherings. My nieces were right there, holding onto me, trying to be so sweet and comforting. And I just stood there, behaving properly, going through the motions. There was just no warmth, no relief. It's like the affectionate part of me has just shut down completely. \n\nWhich is frustrating, because I usually have a very good handle on my emotional intelligence. It's crucial in my role. You can't lead a company if you can't read the room. Just last month, before all this happened, we had this massive town hall meeting to announce the restructuring of our logistics department. People were incredibly anxious, obviously, because restructuring usually means layoffs. I had to stand up there on stage for two hours, answering questions, projecting confidence but also empathy. I actually hired an executive communications coach a few years ago—this brilliant woman who used to work in political speechwriting—and she taught me this technique for grounding yourself before public speaking. You focus on the physical sensation of your feet on the floor. It sounds silly, but it works wonders for centering your energy. I used it right before that town hall, and I was able to connect with the staff, reassure them that our severance packages were well above industry standard, and actually keep morale relatively stable. You have to be present for that kind of thing. You have to feel the energy in the room. \n\nBut now? Now I just feel... nothing. Just quiet and numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in the things you used to do?\n\nPatient: Less interest? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still have the same interests. I still want to do the things I normally do. It's just... well, like I was saying, the feeling isn't there. But the interest itself hasn't changed. \n\nActually, speaking of interests, I was just looking at my golf clubs in the garage yesterday. I've been trying to lower my handicap for the last three years. It's incredibly difficult when you travel as much as I do. I usually only get to play on weekends, and even then, I'm often fielding calls from our European offices. But I joined this fantastic club just outside the city—it's got a beautiful 18-hole course designed by Robert Trent Jones. The greens fees are exorbitant, but the networking is unparalleled. I actually closed a major distribution deal on the 14th hole last summer. The guy I was playing with, he was the VP of operations for a major retail chain, and he had this terrible slice. I gave him a tiny tip on his grip—just a minor adjustment—and he hit the best drive of his life. He was so thrilled, he practically signed the contract right there in the golf cart. It's amazing how much business gets done when people are relaxed. I was supposed to play in a charity tournament there next weekend, actually. The hospital foundation is hosting it. I'm on the advisory board, so I really should make an appearance... \n\nBut anyway, no, my interests are exactly the same. I still want to do everything I usually do. I just... I just feel numb while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy doing are less interesting to you now?\n\nPatient: Less interesting? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? The interest is still there. I still want to do the things I usually do. Like the golf tournament I mentioned, I still want to go. I just don't feel anything when I do it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to have lunch with my college roommate on Tuesday. We've been doing this first-Tuesday-of-the-month lunch for almost twenty years now. We always go to this little Italian place downtown. It's nothing fancy, but they make this incredible truffle risotto that is just to die for. The owner, Marco, always saves us our favorite booth in the back corner so we can talk without everyone hearing us. We usually spend a solid two hours just catching up on everything—our kids, work, the latest drama with her sister. Her sister is constantly getting involved in these ridiculous multi-level marketing schemes. Last year it was essential oils, and she tried to pressure everyone in the family into buying these huge starter kits. We spent half of our lunch just laughing about it. I was actually really looking forward to seeing her this week, to hear what the sister is up to now... \n\nBut, yeah, no. My interests haven't changed at all. The things I enjoy are still interesting to me. I just... I just feel completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day, since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad? No... no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Like I keep saying, it's just this... this numbness. Blankness. Sadness would actually be a feeling, wouldn't it? I just don't have that right now. \n\nIt's strange, actually, because my cousin and I were very close when we were younger. We used to spend every summer at our grandparents' lake house in Michigan. Oh, you should have seen that place. It had this enormous wrap-around porch with these old, creaky rocking chairs. My grandfather built the dock himself back in the sixties. We would sit out there for hours, just dangling our feet in the water, trying to catch sunfish with little pieces of bread. We had this neighbor, Mr. Henderson, who had this beautiful vintage wooden speedboat—a Chris-Craft, I think it was. He was so particular about it, always polishing the mahogany. He'd take us out for rides sometimes, and we felt like absolute royalty zooming across the water. We actually tried to buy that boat from his estate when he passed away, but his son decided to keep it, which I suppose makes sense. But anyway, those summers were just idyllic. You'd think I'd be weeping constantly, given all those memories. But... no. No sadness. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the time? Doctor, I... It's just a blank. \n\nI actually had my performance review at work just a few days before everything happened. The board was extremely pleased with the quarterly numbers. We managed to cut operational costs by almost fifteen percent without laying off a single employee, which I'm quite proud of. It was mostly about restructuring the supply chain. We found this new logistics partner out of Atlanta—fascinating company, actually. They use this proprietary AI algorithm to optimize trucking routes in real-time based on weather and traffic patterns. The CEO is this brilliant young woman who started the company out of her dorm room at Georgia Tech. We had dinner at this incredible steakhouse when she flew in to sign the contract. They had a wine list that was practically the size of a phone book. I ordered a bottle of Silver Oak Cabernet—the 2014 vintage, which was just exceptional. We spent half the evening talking about her collection of vintage typewriters. She has over fifty of them, can you believe that? \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not sad. I'm just... empty. I had my procurement team run the numbers, and the ROI was undeniable. If you don't track the metrics, you can't manage the outcome, that's what I always tell my department heads... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. You asked what I'm still doing even if I don't enjoy it. \n\nSo, yes, I still go to those prayer gatherings. But like I've been telling you, it's completely hollow. I know I sound like a broken record today, but even when I'm sitting there and my nieces are hugging me and trying so hard to comfort me... I'm just going through the motions. I'm participating in the activity, I'm behaving properly as the matriarch of the family, but that's all it is. I feel absolutely zero warmth or relief from it. It's just... quiet. That whole affectionate part of me is just quiet.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to experience any kind of pleasure at all, even in small things?\n\nPatient: Small things? Like... well, normally I'd say my morning espresso. I had a custom La Marzocco machine installed in the executive suite last year during our floor renovation. The renovation itself was a complete headache, by the way. The interior design firm we contracted kept trying to push this open-concept layout, which I absolutely vetoed. Open concept is terrible for executive confidentiality, and the acoustics are a nightmare for international conference calls. I had to personally step in and redesign the floor plan with the architects to ensure the C-suite had proper soundproofing. We ended up sourcing these incredible acoustic glass panels from a supplier in Germany, which delayed the project by three weeks because of customs, but it was absolutely worth it. You have to stand your ground with contractors or they will just walk all over your vision and stick you with a subpar product... \n\nBut anyway, small things. Pleasure. No. Honestly, it's exactly what I was just telling you a moment ago. I know I'm repeating myself, but whether it's a cup of coffee or those prayer gatherings with my family, the result is the same. Even when my nieces were right there, hugging me and trying to comfort me, I was just behaving properly. Doing what's expected. There's just no warmth or relief inside me at all. It really is like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain has just gone completely quiet. So no, not even the small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you could have done something to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? No, I... I don't see how I could have. He was in another state when the accident happened, and I was in the middle of our quarterly earnings call. It's funny you bring up prevention, actually, because risk management is a huge part of my job. Just last quarter, I had our legal team completely overhaul our vendor liability contracts because one of our suppliers in Southeast Asia was cutting corners on safety protocols. I told the board, you cannot outsource liability. If a factory goes up in flames, the press doesn't care that it was a third-party contractor; they care whose logo is on the shipping crates. It's exactly like I said, that whole affectionate, positive part of me has just gone completely quiet. My nieces were right there, holding onto me, trying to be so sweet and comforting. And I just stood there, behaving properly, going through the motions. There was just no warmth, no relief. It's like the affectionate part of me has just shut down completely. \n\nWhich is frustrating, because I usually have a very good handle on my emotional intelligence. It's crucial in my role. You can't lead a company if you can't read the room. Just last month, before all this happened, we had this massive town hall meeting to announce the restructuring of our logistics department. People were incredibly anxious, obviously, because restructuring usually means layoffs. I had to stand up there on stage for two hours, answering questions, projecting confidence but also empathy. I actually hired an executive communications coach a few years ago—this brilliant woman who used to work in political speechwriting—and she taught me this technique for grounding yourself before public speaking. You focus on the physical sensation of your feet on the floor. It sounds silly, but it works wonders for centering your energy. I used it right before that town hall, and I was able to connect with the staff, reassure them that our severance packages were well above industry standard, and actually keep morale relatively stable. You have to be present for that kind of thing. You have to feel the energy in the room. \n\nBut now? Now I just feel... nothing. Just quiet and numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in the things you used to do?\n\nPatient: Less interest? No, I wouldn't say that. I gave him a tiny tip on his grip—just a minor adjustment—and he hit the best drive of his life. He was so thrilled, he practically signed the contract right there in the golf cart. I'm on the advisory board, so I really should make an appearance... \n\nBut anyway, no, my interests are exactly the same. I still want to do everything I usually do. I just... I just feel numb while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy doing are less interesting to you now?\n\nPatient: Less interesting? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? The interest is still there. I still want to do the things I usually do. Like the golf tournament I mentioned, I still want to go. I just don't feel anything when I do it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to have lunch with my college roommate on Tuesday. It's nothing fancy, but they make this incredible truffle risotto that is just to die for. The owner, Marco, always saves us our favorite booth in the back corner so we can talk without everyone hearing us. I was actually really looking forward to seeing her this week, to hear what the sister is up to now... \n\nBut, yeah, no. My interests haven't changed at all. Doctor, I... honestly, I am starting to feel a little frustrated. We have covered this three times now. I do not feel sad. I don't feel a persistent sense of sadness, or a fleeting sense of sadness, or any kind of sadness. I feel numb. Empty. 

It reminds me of this time I had to get a root canal, actually. Have you ever had one? My dentist, Dr. Aris, he has this office downtown in one of those beautiful old art deco buildings. The elevator still has the original brass operator dial, it's gorgeous. It was just this bizarre, hollow pressure. Dr. Aris is quite a character, by the way. He plays classical guitar in his spare time. He actually performed at a charity gala I organized a few years ago for the local arts council. We raised over two hundred thousand dollars that night, mostly through a silent auction. We had this one item—a weekend stay at a private vineyard in Napa—that sparked a bidding war between two of my board members. It was hilarious to watch, they were practically glaring at each other across the ballroom...

I actually told the mayor at a charity fundraiser last year that the city needs to completely overhaul and digitize their records department, but dealing with local government is like talking to a brick wall. They have absolutely no concept of basic operational efficiency... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, how long I've been feeling this way. Right. Yes, it's been about three weeks, since the night of the collision.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have been able to enjoy or look forward to since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Look forward to? Enjoy? No, I... I really don't think so. I actually had my executive assistant clear my calendar of all the social engagements for the month, which was a massive undertaking. She had to cancel my attendance at the Women in Leadership summit, which I was supposed to keynote. I had this whole speech prepared about navigating corporate restructuring during economic downturns. It's a very timely topic, considering the current interest rates and how supply chain disruptions are forcing companies to pivot their entire distribution models. I was going to use our acquisition of that logistics firm in Atlanta as a case study. We managed to integrate their entire routing software into our mainframe in under ninety days, which was a logistical miracle, frankly. The project manager on that integration, David, he's brilliant, I'm probably going to promote him to VP of Operations by the end of the fiscal year... \n\nBut I'm sorry, you asked about enjoying things. Look forward to things. No. Like I mentioned before, even with my family around... like during the prayers, or when my nieces were trying so hard to be sweet and comfort me... I just couldn't feel it. I went through the motions, I behaved the way you're supposed to behave, but there was just zero warmth or... or relief inside. It's like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain just shut off. So no, there's really nothing I'm enjoying right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find interesting or enjoyable before your cousin's death that you have still been doing, even if you don't enjoy them as much?\n\nPatient: Activities I still do? Well, I suppose I'm still attending the family gatherings and the prayer circles for him. We had to organize quite a few of them. I actually ended up having to coordinate the catering for the last one myself because my sister-in-law was completely overwhelmed. I tried to use my usual corporate caterer—they do these incredible artisanal spreads and are completely zero-waste, which is a major sustainability initiative I've been pushing at the company. I actually mandated that all our executive retreats be zero-waste by Q3. It's not just about optics; the cost savings on disposable materials alone justified the switch within six months. I had my procurement team run the numbers, and the ROI was undeniable. If you don't track the metrics, you can't manage the outcome, that's what I always tell my department heads... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. You asked what I'm still doing even if I don't enjoy it. \n\nSo, yes, I still go to those prayer gatherings. But like I've been telling you, it's completely hollow. I know I sound like a broken record today, but even when I'm sitting there and my nieces are hugging me and trying so hard to comfort me... I'm just going through the motions. I'm participating in the activity, I'm behaving properly as the matriarch of the family, but that's all it is. I feel absolutely zero warmth or relief from it. It's just... quiet. That whole affectionate part of me is just quiet.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to experience any kind of pleasure at all, even in small things?\n\nPatient: Small things? Like... well, normally I'd say my morning espresso. I had a custom La Marzocco machine installed in the executive suite last year during our floor renovation. The renovation itself was a complete headache, by the way. The interior design firm we contracted kept trying to push this open-concept layout, which I absolutely vetoed. Open concept is terrible for executive confidentiality, and the acoustics are a nightmare for international conference calls. I had to personally step in and redesign the floor plan with the architects to ensure the C-suite had proper soundproofing. We ended up sourcing these incredible acoustic glass panels from a supplier in Germany, which delayed the project by three weeks because of customs, but it was absolutely worth it. You have to stand your ground with contractors or they will just walk all over your vision and stick you with a subpar product... \n\nBut anyway, small things. Pleasure. No. Honestly, it's exactly what I was just telling you a moment ago. I know I'm repeating myself, but whether it's a cup of coffee or those prayer gatherings with my family, the result is the same. Even when my nieces were right there, hugging me and trying to comfort me, I was just behaving properly. Doing what's expected. There's just no warmth or relief inside me at all. It really is like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain has just gone completely quiet. So no, not even the small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you could have done something to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? No, I... I don't see how I could have. It's like the affectionate part of me has just shut down completely. \n\nWhich is frustrating, because I usually have a very good handle on my emotional intelligence. It's crucial in my role. People were incredibly anxious, obviously, because restructuring usually means layoffs. I had to stand up there on stage for two hours, answering questions, projecting confidence but also empathy. I actually hired an executive communications coach a few years ago—this brilliant woman who used to work in political speechwriting—and she taught me this technique for grounding yourself before public speaking. I used it right before that town hall, and I was able to connect with the staff, reassure them that our severance packages were well above industry standard, and actually keep morale relatively stable. You have to be present for that kind of thing. You have to feel the energy in the room. \n\nBut now? Now I just feel... nothing. Just quiet and numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in the things you used to do?\n\nPatient: Less interest? No, I wouldn't say that. But I joined this fantastic club just outside the city—it's got a beautiful 18-hole course designed by Robert Trent Jones. The greens fees are exorbitant, but the networking is unparalleled. I actually closed a major distribution deal on the 14th hole last summer. The guy I was playing with, he was the VP of operations for a major retail chain, and he had this terrible slice. I gave him a tiny tip on his grip—just a minor adjustment—and he hit the best drive of his life. He was so thrilled, he practically signed the contract right there in the golf cart. It's amazing how much business gets done when people are relaxed. I was supposed to play in a charity tournament there next weekend, actually. The hospital foundation is hosting it. I'm on the advisory board, so I really should make an appearance... \n\nBut anyway, no, my interests are exactly the same. I still want to do everything I usually do. I just... I just feel numb while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy doing are less interesting to you now?\n\nPatient: Less interesting? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? The interest is still there. I still want to do the things I usually do. Like the golf tournament I mentioned, I still want to go. I just don't feel anything when I do it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to have lunch with my college roommate on Tuesday. We've been doing this first-Tuesday-of-the-month lunch for almost twenty years now. We always go to this little Italian place downtown. It's nothing fancy, but they make this incredible truffle risotto that is just to die for. The owner, Marco, always saves us our favorite booth in the back corner so we can talk without everyone hearing us. We usually spend a solid two hours just catching up on everything—our kids, work, the latest drama with her sister. Her sister is constantly getting involved in these ridiculous multi-level marketing schemes. Last year it was essential oils, and she tried to pressure everyone in the family into buying these huge starter kits. We spent half of our lunch just laughing about it. I was actually really looking forward to seeing her this week, to hear what the sister is up to now... \n\nBut, yeah, no. My interests haven't changed at all. The things I enjoy are still interesting to me. I just... I just feel completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day, since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad? No... no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Like I keep saying, it's just this... this numbness. Blankness. Sadness would actually be a feeling, wouldn't it? I just don't have that right now. \n\nIt's strange, actually, because my cousin and I were very close when we were younger. We used to spend every summer at our grandparents' lake house in Michigan. Oh, you should have seen that place. It had this enormous wrap-around porch with these old, creaky rocking chairs. My grandfather built the dock himself back in the sixties. We would sit out there for hours, just dangling our feet in the water, trying to catch sunfish with little pieces of bread. We had this neighbor, Mr. Henderson, who had this beautiful vintage wooden speedboat—a Chris-Craft, I think it was. He was so particular about it, always polishing the mahogany. He'd take us out for rides sometimes, and we felt like absolute royalty zooming across the water. We actually tried to buy that boat from his estate when he passed away, but his son decided to keep it, which I suppose makes sense. But anyway, those summers were just idyllic. You'd think I'd be weeping constantly, given all those memories. But... no. No sadness. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the time? Doctor, I... I feel like I'm repeating myself here. We just talked about this. No, I don't feel sad. I don't feel anything. It's just a blank. \n\nI actually had my performance review at work just a few days before everything happened. The board was extremely pleased with the quarterly numbers. We managed to cut operational costs by almost fifteen percent without laying off a single employee, which I'm quite proud of. It was mostly about restructuring the supply chain. We found this new logistics partner out of Atlanta—fascinating company, actually. They use this proprietary AI algorithm to optimize trucking routes in real-time based on weather and traffic patterns. The CEO is this brilliant young woman who started the company out of her dorm room at Georgia Tech. We had dinner at this incredible steakhouse when she flew in to sign the contract. They had a wine list that was practically the size of a phone book. I ordered a bottle of Silver Oak Cabernet—the 2014 vintage, which was just exceptional. We spent half the evening talking about her collection of vintage typewriters. She has over fifty of them, can you believe that? \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not sad. I'm just... empty. I don't know how many different ways I can say it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy are now feeling uninteresting or unpleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... are you reading from a list? She's been entirely obsessed with animals since she was a toddler. When she was about seven, she actually tried to smuggle a stray iguana into my sister's house in her school backpack. An iguana! My sister opened the bag to check her math homework and this green, scaly thing just darted right across the kitchen island. She screamed so loud the neighbors actually called the police. The officer who showed up—Officer Davis, I think his name was, he used to patrol that area all the time—he actually had to help them catch the lizard with a pool skimmer. Empty? Yes. Yes, that's exactly what I've been trying to tell you. Empty. 

Like I was saying before about the prayer gatherings for my cousin, and when my nieces came over... I was doing all the right things, saying the right things, behaving exactly how a person is supposed to behave in a time of mourning. But inside? Nothing. Absolutely no warmth, no relief from their comfort. It's like the affectionate part of my brain just... switched off. Went completely quiet. 

It actually feels a bit like when my company's main server went down last quarter. We have this massive data center, climate-controlled, backup generators, the whole nine yards. We spend a fortune on IT infrastructure. Our head of IT, Marcus, he's a brilliant guy—used to work for the Pentagon, actually, doing cybersecurity. He's got this fascinating background, speaks fluent Russian and Mandarian. He was telling me once about how they used to track these sophisticated hacking syndicates... but anyway, the server just went down. One minute everything is humming along, processing millions of transactions, and the next minute... just a blank screen. No error message, no warning lights, just nothing. We had to fly in a specialist from Seattle to figure out what happened. Turned out to be a microscopic flaw in one of the motherboards that just finally gave out. The specialist, she was amazing. She actually brought her own custom-built diagnostic tools in these sleek aluminum cases that looked like they belonged in a James Bond movie. I asked her where she got them, and she said she machines the parts herself in her garage on weekends. She also restores vintage motorcycles—specifically Triumphs from the 1960s. 

But yes, empty. That's the word. Just... quiet and empty.

I had my procurement team run the numbers, and the ROI was undeniable. If you don't track the metrics, you can't manage the outcome, that's what I always tell my department heads... \n\nBut, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. You asked what I'm still doing even if I don't enjoy it. \n\nSo, yes, I still go to those prayer gatherings. But like I've been telling you, it's completely hollow. I know I sound like a broken record today, but even when I'm sitting there and my nieces are hugging me and trying so hard to comfort me... I'm just going through the motions. I'm participating in the activity, I'm behaving properly as the matriarch of the family, but that's all it is. I feel absolutely zero warmth or relief from it. It's just... quiet. That whole affectionate part of me is just quiet.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to experience any kind of pleasure at all, even in small things?\n\nPatient: Small things? Like... well, normally I'd say my morning espresso. I had a custom La Marzocco machine installed in the executive suite last year during our floor renovation. The renovation itself was a complete headache, by the way. The interior design firm we contracted kept trying to push this open-concept layout, which I absolutely vetoed. Open concept is terrible for executive confidentiality, and the acoustics are a nightmare for international conference calls. I had to personally step in and redesign the floor plan with the architects to ensure the C-suite had proper soundproofing. We ended up sourcing these incredible acoustic glass panels from a supplier in Germany, which delayed the project by three weeks because of customs, but it was absolutely worth it. You have to stand your ground with contractors or they will just walk all over your vision and stick you with a subpar product... \n\nBut anyway, small things. Pleasure. No. Honestly, it's exactly what I was just telling you a moment ago. I know I'm repeating myself, but whether it's a cup of coffee or those prayer gatherings with my family, the result is the same. Even when my nieces were right there, hugging me and trying to comfort me, I was just behaving properly. Doing what's expected. There's just no warmth or relief inside me at all. It really is like that whole affectionate, positive part of my brain has just gone completely quiet. So no, not even the small things.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you could have done something to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Prevent it? No, I... I don't see how I could have. He was in another state when the accident happened, and I was in the middle of our quarterly earnings call. It's funny you bring up prevention, actually, because risk management is a huge part of my job. Just last quarter, I had our legal team completely overhaul our vendor liability contracts because one of our suppliers in Southeast Asia was cutting corners on safety protocols. I told the board, you cannot outsource liability. If a factory goes up in flames, the press doesn't care that it was a third-party contractor; they care whose logo is on the shipping crates. We ended up switching to a more expensive supplier in Vietnam, which took a hit on our margins in Q2, but the long-term risk mitigation is invaluable. I'm actually flying out there next month to personally audit their facilities, assuming I can get my calendar sorted out... \n\nBut no, regarding my cousin. I don't have those kinds of thoughts. I know I couldn't have stopped it. It was just a terrible accident.\n\nDoctor: Are you able to feel any pleasure or enjoyment when you are doing things that you normally would find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Pleasure or enjoyment? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? I know my mind has been a bit scattered lately, but I'm pretty sure I just said... \n\nLook, it's the same thing I was just explaining about the prayer gatherings. It doesn't matter what the activity is. Even when my nieces were right there, literally wrapping their arms around me and trying so hard to comfort me, I felt absolutely nothing. I was just... behaving properly. Doing the right things, saying the right things, but feeling zero warmth or relief inside. It's exactly like I said, that whole affectionate, positive part of me has just gone completely quiet. So no, I'm not feeling any enjoyment, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel empty or numb inside most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Empty or numb? Yes, I suppose that's the best way to describe it. Like I was just saying... I feel like a broken record here, but it's exactly what happened at those prayer gatherings. My nieces were right there, holding onto me, trying to be so sweet and comforting. And I just stood there, behaving properly, going through the motions. There was just no warmth, no relief. It's like the affectionate part of me has just shut down completely. \n\nWhich is frustrating, because I usually have a very good handle on my emotional intelligence. It's crucial in my role. You can't lead a company if you can't read the room. Just last month, before all this happened, we had this massive town hall meeting to announce the restructuring of our logistics department. People were incredibly anxious, obviously, because restructuring usually means layoffs. I had to stand up there on stage for two hours, answering questions, projecting confidence but also empathy. I actually hired an executive communications coach a few years ago—this brilliant woman who used to work in political speechwriting—and she taught me this technique for grounding yourself before public speaking. You focus on the physical sensation of your feet on the floor. It sounds silly, but it works wonders for centering your energy. I used it right before that town hall, and I was able to connect with the staff, reassure them that our severance packages were well above industry standard, and actually keep morale relatively stable. You have to be present for that kind of thing. You have to feel the energy in the room. \n\nBut now? Now I just feel... nothing. Just quiet and numb inside.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that you have less interest in the things you used to do?\n\nPatient: Less interest? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still have the same interests. I still want to do the things I normally do. It's just... well, like I was saying, the feeling isn't there. But the interest itself hasn't changed. \n\nActually, speaking of interests, I was just looking at my golf clubs in the garage yesterday. I've been trying to lower my handicap for the last three years. It's incredibly difficult when you travel as much as I do. I usually only get to play on weekends, and even then, I'm often fielding calls from our European offices. But I joined this fantastic club just outside the city—it's got a beautiful 18-hole course designed by Robert Trent Jones. The greens fees are exorbitant, but the networking is unparalleled. I actually closed a major distribution deal on the 14th hole last summer. The guy I was playing with, he was the VP of operations for a major retail chain, and he had this terrible slice. I gave him a tiny tip on his grip—just a minor adjustment—and he hit the best drive of his life. He was so thrilled, he practically signed the contract right there in the golf cart. It's amazing how much business gets done when people are relaxed. I was supposed to play in a charity tournament there next weekend, actually. The hospital foundation is hosting it. I'm on the advisory board, so I really should make an appearance... \n\nBut anyway, no, my interests are exactly the same. I still want to do everything I usually do. I just... I just feel numb while I'm doing them.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy doing are less interesting to you now?\n\nPatient: Less interesting? I... I feel like we just covered this, didn't we? The interest is still there. I still want to do the things I usually do. Like the golf tournament I mentioned, I still want to go. I just don't feel anything when I do it. \n\nI mean, I was supposed to have lunch with my college roommate on Tuesday. We've been doing this first-Tuesday-of-the-month lunch for almost twenty years now. We always go to this little Italian place downtown. It's nothing fancy, but they make this incredible truffle risotto that is just to die for. The owner, Marco, always saves us our favorite booth in the back corner so we can talk without everyone hearing us. We usually spend a solid two hours just catching up on everything—our kids, work, the latest drama with her sister. Her sister is constantly getting involved in these ridiculous multi-level marketing schemes. Last year it was essential oils, and she tried to pressure everyone in the family into buying these huge starter kits. We spent half of our lunch just laughing about it. I was actually really looking forward to seeing her this week, to hear what the sister is up to now... \n\nBut, yeah, no. My interests haven't changed at all. The things I enjoy are still interesting to me. I just... I just feel completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day, every day, since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Sad? No... no, I wouldn't say I feel sad. Like I keep saying, it's just this... this numbness. Blankness. Sadness would actually be a feeling, wouldn't it? I just don't have that right now. \n\nIt's strange, actually, because my cousin and I were very close when we were younger. We used to spend every summer at our grandparents' lake house in Michigan. Oh, you should have seen that place. It had this enormous wrap-around porch with these old, creaky rocking chairs. My grandfather built the dock himself back in the sixties. We would sit out there for hours, just dangling our feet in the water, trying to catch sunfish with little pieces of bread. We had this neighbor, Mr. Henderson, who had this beautiful vintage wooden speedboat—a Chris-Craft, I think it was. He was so particular about it, always polishing the mahogany. He'd take us out for rides sometimes, and we felt like absolute royalty zooming across the water. We actually tried to buy that boat from his estate when he passed away, but his son decided to keep it, which I suppose makes sense. But anyway, those summers were just idyllic. You'd think I'd be weeping constantly, given all those memories. But... no. No sadness. Just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Sad most of the time? Doctor, I... I feel like I'm repeating myself here. We just talked about this. No, I don't feel sad. I don't feel anything. It's just a blank. \n\nI actually had my performance review at work just a few days before everything happened. The board was extremely pleased with the quarterly numbers. We managed to cut operational costs by almost fifteen percent without laying off a single employee, which I'm quite proud of. It was mostly about restructuring the supply chain. We found this new logistics partner out of Atlanta—fascinating company, actually. They use this proprietary AI algorithm to optimize trucking routes in real-time based on weather and traffic patterns. The CEO is this brilliant young woman who started the company out of her dorm room at Georgia Tech. We had dinner at this incredible steakhouse when she flew in to sign the contract. They had a wine list that was practically the size of a phone book. I ordered a bottle of Silver Oak Cabernet—the 2014 vintage, which was just exceptional. We spent half the evening talking about her collection of vintage typewriters. She has over fifty of them, can you believe that? \n\nBut anyway, no. I'm not sad. I'm just... empty. I don't know how many different ways I can say it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that the things you used to enjoy are now feeling uninteresting or unpleasurable to you?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... are you reading from a list? Because I really feel like we're just going in circles here. Like I said, the interest is there, but the feeling just isn't. For instance, during the prayer gatherings for my cousin, and even when my nieces came over to try and comfort me... I found myself behaving properly, doing exactly what you're supposed to do in those situations, but feeling absolutely no warmth or relief inside. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone completely quiet. \n\nMy nieces are wonderful girls, though. The older one, Sarah, actually just got accepted into a veterinary medicine program out in Colorado. Fort Collins, I believe. It's a highly competitive program, so the family is very proud of her. She's been entirely obsessed with animals since she was a toddler. When she was about seven, she actually tried to smuggle a stray iguana into my sister's house in her school backpack. An iguana! My sister opened the bag to check her math homework and this green, scaly thing just darted right across the kitchen island. She screamed so loud the neighbors actually called the police. The officer who showed up—Officer Davis, I think his name was, he used to patrol that area all the time—he actually had to help them catch the lizard with a pool skimmer. He had this magnificent German Shepherd in his cruiser, just a beautifully trained animal. I've always admired working dogs like that. The discipline it takes to train them is really quite something. I read an article in Forbes last year about how the military is using virtual reality to help train... \n\nWait, what was the question again? Unpleasurable? Right. Yes. Like I said, I just... there's no warmth right now. Just quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a persistent sense of sadness inside you right now?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... honestly, I am starting to feel a little frustrated. We have covered this three times now. I do not feel sad. I don't feel a persistent sense of sadness, or a fleeting sense of sadness, or any kind of sadness. I feel numb. Empty. \n\nIt reminds me of this time I had to get a root canal, actually. Have you ever had one? My dentist, Dr. Aris, he has this office downtown in one of those beautiful old art deco buildings. The elevator still has the original brass operator dial, it's gorgeous. Anyway, he gave me the novocaine, and I remember sitting there in the chair, watching him work with all those tiny little files and mirrors, knowing he was drilling right into the nerve of my tooth, but I couldn't feel a single thing. It was just this bizarre, hollow pressure. Dr. Aris is quite a character, by the way. He plays classical guitar in his spare time. Just... novocaine for the soul, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel empty most of the time since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Empty? Yes. Yes, that's exactly what I've been trying to tell you. Empty. \n\nLike I was saying before about the prayer gatherings for my cousin, and when my nieces came over... I was doing all the right things, saying the right things, behaving exactly how a person is supposed to behave in a time of mourning. But inside? Nothing. Absolutely no warmth, no relief from their comfort. It's like the affectionate part of my brain just... switched off. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Three weeks. It's been about three weeks now, ever since the night of the highway collision when... well, when he died. Just three weeks of this complete blankness. 

Highway collisions are just so devastating, aren't they? I actually drive that same stretch of interstate every Tuesday when I go out to our regional office in Springfield. I remember this one night, my roommate—she was a pre-med student, incredibly brilliant, ended up becoming a pediatric neurosurgeon in Chicago... 

I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Three weeks. Yes. Since the accident.
